Decomposition of some Witten–Reshetikhin–Turaev Representations into Irreducible Factors
Julien KORINMAN

Abstract
We decompose into irreducible factors the SU ⁡ ( 2 ) {\rm SU}(2) Witten–Reshetikhin–Turaev representations of the mapping class group of a genus 2 2 surface when the level is p = 4 ​ r p=4r and p = 2 ​ r 2 p=2r^{2} with r r an odd prime and when p = 2 ​ r 1 ​ r 2 p=2r_{1}r_{2} with r 1 r_{1} , r 2 r_{2} two distinct odd primes. Some partial generalizations in higher genus are also presented.



keywords
Witten–Reshetikhin–Turaev representations; mapping class group; topological quantum field theory

AMS
57R56; 57M60

1
Introduction

Witten constructed in
[
14
]
a family of
(2+1)
-dimensional topological quantum field theories (TQFTs) using path integrals and the Chern–Simons action which gives a three-dimensional interpretation of the Jones polynomial. Each of these TQFTs induces a projective finite-dimensional representation of the mapping class group
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})
of a genus
g
closed oriented surface
\Sigma_{g}
. Reshetikhin and Turaev made a rigorous construction of these TQFTs
[
11
]
using representations of quantum groups. In this paper we will follow the skein theoretical construction of
[
2
,
8
]
to define these representations.

The Witten–Reshetikhin–Turaev projective representations lift to linear representations of a central extension
\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g})
of
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})

\displaystyle\rho_{p,g}\colon\ \widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g})\rightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})).

Here
p\geq 6
is an even integer indexing the representations, called the
level
, and
V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
is a finite-dimensional complex vector space. These representations are equipped with an invariant Hermitian non-degenerate form
\langle\,,\,\rangle_{p,g}
.

The goal of this paper is to decompose some of these representations into irreducible factors. There are only few results in that direction. In
[
2
]
, the authors construct an explicit proper invariant submodule of
V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
, when
4
divides
p
. Roberts proved in
[
12
]
that
\rho_{p,g}
is irreducible if
\frac{p}{2}
is an odd prime. An immediate extension of his proof shows that the representations
\rho_{18,g}
are irreducible. In
[
1
]
, Andersen and Fjelstad proved that for
p=24,36,60
, the
\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g})
-module
V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
contains at least three invariant submodules. The author gave in
[
7
]
an explicit decomposition into irreducible factors of the modules
V_{p}(\Sigma_{1})
, arising in genus one, for arbitrary level
p\geq 3
. Note that one can extend the definition of the Witten–Reshetikhin–Turaev representations to mapping class groups of punctured surfaces, which are indexed by some coloring of the punctures in addition to the level. Koberda and Santharoubane showed in
[
6
]
that any representation of a punctured surface with at least one puncture colored by
1
is irreducible.

Given
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
two distinct odd primes, there exists an unique even integer
x=x(r_{1},r_{2})\in\{1,\ldots,r_{1}r_{2}-2\}
such that
x
verifies either

\displaystyle\begin{cases}x\equiv-2&\pmod{r_{1}},\\
x\equiv 0&\pmod{r_{2}},\end{cases}\qquad\text{or}\qquad\begin{cases}x\equiv 0&\pmod{r_{1}},\\
x\equiv-2&\pmod{r_{2}}.\end{cases}

The main result of this paper is the following:

Theorem 1.1
.

1.

The modules
V_{18}(\Sigma_{g})
are simple for
g\geq 2
.

2.

If
r
is an odd prime, then
V_{4r}(\Sigma_{2})
is the sum of two simple submodules.

3.

If
r
is an odd prime, then
V_{2r^{2}}(\Sigma_{2})
is simple.

4.

If
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
are two distinct odd primes such that either
r_{1},r_{2}>37
or the element
x
defined above satisfies
3x>2r_{1}r_{2}-4
. Then
V_{2r_{1}r_{2}}(\Sigma_{2})
is simple.

The main obstruction to extend the above theorem to higher genus is that we need to control which
6j
-symbols vanish. In the last section we will state a conjecture concerning the vanishing
6j
-symbols. We will then prove that this conjecture implies a generalization of Theorem
1.1
in higher genus. The author verified numerically the conjecture for small levels from which we deduce the:

Theorem 1.2
.

1.

Each module
V_{12}(\Sigma_{3})
,
V_{20}(\Sigma_{3})
,
V_{28}(\Sigma_{3})
,
V_{44}(\Sigma_{3})
,
V_{52}(\Sigma_{3})
is the direct sum of two simple submodules.

2.

For any
g\geq 3
, the modules
V_{30}(\Sigma_{g})
,
V_{66}(\Sigma_{g})
are simple.

The proof of Theorem
1.2
relies on a numerical computation of the
6j
-symbols up to level
66
.

Remark 1.3
.

In
[
2
]
some representations
\rho_{p,g}
are also defined when
p
is odd. They verify
\rho_{2p,g}\cong\rho_{p,g}\otimes\rho^{\prime}_{2,g}
. In particular if an odd level
r
is such that
V_{2r}(\Sigma_{g})
is simple, then
V_{r}(\Sigma_{g})
is also simple. So our theorems extend to
{\rm SO}(3)
cases as well.

2
Skein construction of the Witten–Reshetikhin–Turaev

representations

Following
[
2
]
, we will briefly define the Witten–Reshetikhin–Turaev representations and fix some notations.

2.1
The spaces
\boldsymbol{V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})}

Given an even integer
p\geq 6
, we denote by
A\in\mathbb{C}
an arbitrary primitive
2p
-th root of unity. Given a compact oriented
3
-manifold
M
, a
framed link with
n
components

L\subset M
is an isotopy class of an embedding of the disjoint union of
n
copies of
S^{1}\times[0,1]
into
M
. Using the Kauffman-bracket skein relation of Fig.
1
, we associate to any framed link
L\subset S^{3}
an invariant
\langle L\rangle_{p}\in\mathbb{C}
.

Figure 1:
The Kauffman-bracket skein relations defining the framed links invariant.

Let
g\geq 1
and denote by
\Sigma_{g}
a compact oriented surface of genus
g
and by
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})
the mapping class group of
\Sigma_{g}
, namely the group of isotopy classes of orientation preserving homeomorphisms of
\Sigma_{g}
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{g}
represents the set of isotopy classes of framed links (including the empty link) in an oriented genus
g
handlebody
H_{g}
. We fix a genus
g
Heegaard splitting of the sphere, i.e., two homeomorphisms
S\colon\partial H_{g}\cong\overline{\partial H_{g}}
and
H_{g}\bigcup_{S\colon\partial H_{g}\rightarrow\overline{\partial H_{g}}}H_{g}\cong S^{3}
. For instance, define the handlebody
H_{g}=\big(D^{2}\times S^{1}\big)^{\#g}
as a connected sums of
g
copies of
D^{2}\times S^{1}
. Consider two oriented curves
L,M\subset\partial\big(D^{2}\times S^{1}\big)
intersecting once positively. Denote by
L_{i},M_{i}\subset\partial H_{g}
,
i=1,\ldots,g
the images of
L
and
M
respectively in the
i
-th connected component of
H_{g}
. One can choose the homeomorphism
S\colon\partial H_{g}\rightarrow H_{g}
such that the image of the curve
L_{i}
is
M_{i}
for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,g\}
. The mapping class of
S
is uniquely determined by this condition and we have a homeomorphism
H_{g}\bigcup_{S}H_{g}\cong S^{3}
. We denote by
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\colon H_{g}\hookrightarrow S^{3}
the embeddings in the first and second factors.

Choose
L_{1},L_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{g}
. The above gluing defines a link
\varphi_{1}(L_{1})\bigcup\varphi_{2}(L_{2})\subset S^{3}
. The
Hopf pairing
is the Hermitian form

\displaystyle(\cdot,\cdot)_{g,p}^{H}\colon\ \mathbb{C}[\mathcal{C}_{g}]\times\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{C}_{g}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}

defined by

\displaystyle(L_{1},L_{2}t)_{g,p}^{H}:=\left\langle\varphi_{1}(L_{1})\bigcup\varphi_{2}(L_{2})\right\rangle_{p}.

Next we define the spaces
V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
as the quotients:

\displaystyle V_{p}(\Sigma_{g}):={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{C}_{g}]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\ker\big((\cdot,\cdot)_{g,p}^{H}\big)$}\right.}.

It is proved in
[
2
]
that the vector spaces
V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
are finite dimensional. Let us provide an explicit basis as follows. Given
g\geq 2
, choose a trivalent banded graph
\Gamma\subset H_{g}
such that
H_{g}
retracts on
\Gamma
by deformation. By banded graph we mean a thickening of the graph by an oriented surface. If
g=1
,
\Gamma
represents the band
S^{1}\times\big[{-}\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\big]\subset S^{1}\times D^{2}\cong H_{1}
. We denote by
E(\Gamma)
the set of edges of
\Gamma
. Set
I_{p}:=\big\{0,\ldots,\frac{p-4}{2}\big\}
, which will be called the set of
colors
at level
p
.

A triple of colors
i,j,k\in I_{p}
is said
p
-admissible
if:

1)

|i-j|\leq k\leq i+j
,

2)

i+j+k
is even and
i+j+k\leq p-4
.

A map
\sigma\colon E(\Gamma)\rightarrow I_{p}
is a
p
-admissible coloring
of
\Gamma
if for every three edges
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\in E(\Gamma)
adjacent to a vertex, the triple
(\sigma(e_{1}),\sigma(e_{2}),\sigma(e_{3}))
is
p
-admissible.

In
[
5
,
13
]
, the authors defined some idempotents
\big\{f_{0},\ldots,f_{\frac{p-4}{2}}\big\}
of the Temperley–Lieb algebra with coefficient in
\mathbb{Q}(A)
called
the Jones–Wenzl idempotents
. To every
p
-admissible coloring
\sigma
of
\Gamma
, we associate a vector
u_{\sigma}\in V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
as follows. Replace each edge
e\in E(\Gamma)
by the Jones–Wenzl idempotent
f_{\sigma(e)}
. If
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})
are three edges adjacent to a vertex of
\Gamma
, we connect the adjacent idempotents using the link
T_{\sigma(e_{1}),\sigma(e_{2}),\sigma(e_{3})}
drawn in Fig.
2
.

Figure 2:
The link
T_{i,j,k}
used to connect three idempotents
f_{i}
,
f_{j}
and
f_{k}
. The number above each three arcs denotes the number of parallel copies of the arc used to define the framed link.

Theorem 4.11 of
[
2
]
asserts that the vectors
u_{\sigma}
, where
\sigma
belongs to the set of
p
-admissible colorings of
\Gamma
, form a basis of
V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
.

The basis
u_{\sigma}
depends on the choice of the embedded banded trivalent graph. One can transform any trivalent graph into any other one by a sequence of Whitehead moves and twists. We say that two banded trivalent graphs
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
embedded in a handlebody differ by a Whitehead move if there exists a ball
B^{3}
intersecting each
\Gamma_{i}
transversally in four edges such that the two banded graphs coincide outside the ball and such that their intersection with
B^{3}
is as drawn in Fig.
3
.

Figure 3:
The two graphs
\Gamma_{1}
on the left and
\Gamma_{2}
on the right differ by a local Whitehead move.

Fix a
p
-admissible coloring of the graph outside
B^{3}
with the four edges intersecting
B^{3}
colored by
a
,
b
,
c
and
d
. Denote by
i
a
b
c
d
and
j
a
b
c
d
the vectors associated to the coloring of
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
respectively with the edge inside
B^{3}
colored by
i
and
j
respectively.

Lemma 2.1

(fusion-rule
[
10
]
)
.

The vector
i
a
b
c
d
belongs to the sub-space spanned by the vectors
j
a
b
c
d
and decomposes as follows

\displaystyle\hbox to49.83pt{\vbox to30.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 12.1519pt\lower-6.80522pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where the sum runs through
p
-admissible colorings.

In this formula, the coefficient
\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}a&b&j\\
c&d&i\end{array}\right\}
only depends on the colors
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
,
i
and
j
and is called
recoupling coefficient
or
6j
-symbol
. We refer to
[
10
]
for a proof and an explicit computation of these coefficients. It follows from the formulas in
[
10
]
that if one of the colors
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
,
i
or
j
is null, then the recoupling coefficient is nonzero.

2.2
The Witten–Reshetikhin–Turaev representations

We fix an orientation preserving homeomorphism
\alpha\colon\Sigma_{g}\rightarrow\partial H_{g}
.

Let
\phi\in\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})
be a mapping class associated to a homeomorphism of
\Sigma_{g}
which extends to a homeomorphism
\widetilde{\phi}\colon H_{g}\rightarrow H_{g}
through
\alpha
, that is such that
\phi=\alpha\circ\widetilde{\phi}_{|\partial H_{g}}\circ\alpha^{-1}
. Then
\widetilde{\phi}
acts on
C_{g}
and preserves the kernel of the Hopf pairing so acts on
V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
by passing to the quotient. Denote by
\tilde{\rho}_{p,g}(\phi)\in\operatorname{GL}(V_{p}(\Sigma_{g}))
the resulting operator. For instance, the Dehn twist along any curve
\gamma\subset\Sigma_{g}
whose image
\alpha(\gamma)
is contractible in
H_{g}
, extends through
\alpha
.

Let
\phi\in\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})
be the mapping class of a homeomorphism which extends to
H_{g}
through
\alpha\circ S
. This extension also defines, by quotient, an operator on
V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
. We denote by
\tilde{\rho}_{p,g}(\phi)
the dual of this operator for the Hopf pairing.

The elements of
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})
which extend to
H_{g}
either through
\alpha
or through
\alpha\circ S
, generate the whole group
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})
. It is a non trivial fact that the associated operators
\tilde{\rho}_{p,g}(\phi)
generate a projective representation:

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{p,g}\colon\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})\rightarrow\operatorname{PGL}(V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})).

We consider a central extension
\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g})
of
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})
that lifts the above projective representations to linear ones (see
[
4
,
9
]
):

\displaystyle\rho_{p,g}\colon\ \widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g})\rightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})).

These are the so-called Witten–Reshetikhin–Turaev representations.

The vector space
V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
admits a non-degenerate Hermitian form, denoted
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{p,g}
and called the
invariant form
, which is invariant under the action of
\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g})
. Moreover, for any trivalent banded graph, the basis associated to its
p
-admissible coloring is orthogonal for this form. The invariant form
\left<\cdot,\cdot\right>_{p,g}
is related to the Hopf pairing
(\cdot,\cdot)_{p,g}
by the formula
(v_{1},v_{2})_{p,g}=\langle v_{1},\rho_{p,g}(S)v_{2}\rangle_{p,g}
for any
v_{1},v_{2}\in V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
.

For each edge
e\in E(\Gamma)
consider a disc
D_{e}
properly embedded in
H_{g}
that intersects
\Gamma
transversely once in a point of the edge
e
. Note that the boundary curves
\gamma_{e}:=\partial D_{e}\subset\partial H_{g}\cong\Sigma_{g}
form a pants decomposition of
\Sigma_{g}
. Here and henceforth
T_{e}\in\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})
denotes the Dehn twist along
\gamma_{e}
.

From a classical property of the Jones–Wenzl idempotents, the authors of
[
2
]
proved that

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{p,g}(T_{e})\cdot u_{\sigma}=\mu_{\sigma(e)}u_{\sigma},

where
\mu_{i}:=(-1)^{i}A^{i(i+2)}
for every
i\in I_{p}
.

We fix the lift of
T_{e}
in
\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g})
, still denoted
T_{e}
, so that
\rho_{p,g}(T_{e})\cdot u_{\sigma}=\mu_{\sigma(e)}u_{\sigma}
.

We also fix the lift
S\in\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g})
so that the matrix of
\rho_{p,g}(S)
is the matrix of the Hopf pairing
(\cdot,\cdot)^{H}_{p,g}
multiplied by the scalar
\eta:=\frac{1}{2p}(A\kappa)^{3}\big(A^{2}-A^{-2}\big)\sum\limits_{m=1}^{2p}{(-1)^{m}A^{-m^{2}}}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
where
\kappa
satisfies
\kappa^{6}=A^{-6-\frac{p(p+1)}{2}}
. The scalar
\eta
represents the sphere invariant in TQFT. We refer to
[
2
]
for a detailed discussion on
\eta
.

Note that
S
and the
T_{e},e\in E(\Gamma)
generate
\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g})
for any trivalent banded graph. Therefore, the above formulas determine uniquely the linear representation
\rho_{p,g}
.

Another description is derived as follows. Recall that
\mathcal{C}_{g}
represents the set of isotopy classes of framed links in
H_{g}
and denote by
\mathcal{K}_{g}
the set of isotopy classes of framed links in
\Sigma_{g}\times[0,1]
. The vector space
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{K}_{g}]
has a natural algebra structure whose product is given by gluing two copies of
\Sigma_{g}\times[0,1]
. Moreover the homeomorphism
\alpha
defines a structure of
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{K}_{g}]
left-module on
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{C}_{g}]
by gluing
\Sigma_{g}\times[0,1]
to
H_{g}
. By passing to the quotient, we obtain a surjective map

\displaystyle\operatorname{Add}_{p}\colon\ \mathbb{C}[\mathcal{K}_{g}]\rightarrow\operatorname{End}(V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})).

If
K
is a framed knot in an oriented
3
manifold and
n\geq 0
, we note
K^{n}
the framed link made of
n
parallel copies of
K
. Recall that the framing is defined as a thickening of the link in an orientable surface. Pushing the link along the direction normal to the thickening defines the notion of parallelism. If
P(X)=\sum_{i}a_{i}X^{i}\in\mathbb{C}[X]
is a polynomial and
K\in\mathcal{C}_{g}
(resp
K\in\mathcal{K}_{g})
is a framed knot, we define
K(P):=\sum_{i}a_{i}K^{i}
in
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{C}_{g}]
(resp in
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{K}_{g}]
) and we call
K(P)
the
framed knot
K
colored by
P
.

Consider
\gamma\subset\Sigma_{g}
a non-contractible oriented simple closed curve. Define
\gamma^{+}\subset\Sigma_{g}\times[0,1]
the framed knot defined by
\gamma\subset\Sigma_{g}\times\big\{\frac{1}{2}\big\}
endowed with the framing given by a normal vector field making one turn in the counter-clockwise direction when circling once along
\gamma
following the orientation. Define
\omega(X)=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{\frac{p-4}{2}}(-1)^{i}[i+1]S_{i}(X)\in\mathbb{C}[X]
, where
S_{i}(X)
represents the
i
-th Chebyshev polynomial of second kind defined by
S_{0}(X)=1
,
S_{1}(X)=X
and
S_{i+2}(X)=XS_{i+1}(X)-S_{i}(X)
and
[n]:=\frac{A^{2n}-A^{-2n}}{A^{2}-A^{-2}}
. Then an important property of the Witten–Reshetikhin–Turaev representations is that the operator
\rho_{p,g}(T_{\gamma})
, associated to the lift of the Dehn twist along
\gamma
, is equal to the operator
\operatorname{Add}_{p}(\gamma^{+}(\omega))
associated to the coloring by
\omega
of
\gamma^{+}
. Since lifts of Dehn twists generate
\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g})
, this property gives an alternative definition of
\rho_{p,g}
.

3
Cyclicity of the vacuum vector

The vector
v_{0}\in V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
, which is the image of the empty link in
H_{g}
, will be called the
vacuum vector
in genus
g
. Denote by
\mathcal{A}_{p,g}
the subalgebra of
\operatorname{End}(V_{p}(\Sigma_{g}))
generated by the operators
\rho_{p,g}(\phi)
for
\phi\in\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g})
. The key ingredient to prove Theorem
1.1
is to show that the vacuum vector is cyclic, i.e., that
\mathcal{A}_{p,g}\cdot v_{0}=V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
.

3.1
Decomposition in genus one of the Weil representations

In
[
7
, Corollary 1.2]
, the author gave an explicit decomposition of the genus one representations into irreducible factors which will be summarized next. We will derive from this decomposition the following:

Lemma 3.1
.

If
p=2r^{2}
or
p=4r
with
r
an odd prime, or
p=2r_{1}r_{2}
with
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
distinct odd primes, then
v_{0}\in V_{p}(\Sigma_{1})
is cyclic.

The genus
1
Weil representations at level
p
, are projective representations
\pi_{p}\colon\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\operatorname{PGL}(U_{p})
where
U_{p}
is a
p
-dimensional complex vector space with a canonical basis
\{e_{i},\,i\in\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}\}
. They are defined on the generators
T:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
S:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
by the projective classes of the operators:

\displaystyle\pi_{p}(S)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\big(A^{-ij}\big)_{i,j\in\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}},\qquad\pi_{p}(T)=\big(A^{i^{2}}\delta_{i,j}\big)_{i,j\in\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}}.

Here the level is an integer
p\geq 2
not necessarily even. When
p
is even,
A
is a primitive
2p
-th root of unity. When
p
is odd,
A
represents a primitive
p
-th root of unity. The decomposition into irreducible factors of the genus one Weil representations is the following:

Theorem 3.2

(
[
7
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

1.

If
a
and
b
are coprime, then
U_{ab}\cong U_{a}\otimes U_{b}
.

2.

If
r
is prime and
n\geq 1
, then there exists some module
W_{r^{n+2}}
such that
U_{r^{n+2}}\cong U_{r^{n}}\oplus W_{r^{n+2}}
.

3.

If
r
is an odd prime, then
U_{r^{2}}\cong\mathds{1}\oplus W_{r^{2}}
where
\mathds{1}
is the one-dimensional trivial representation.

4.

Each one of the modules
U_{p}
and
W_{r^{n}}
is the direct sum of two submodules
U_{p}\cong U_{p}^{-}\oplus U_{p}^{+}
,
W_{r^{n}}\cong W_{r^{n}}^{+}\oplus W_{r^{n}}^{-}
.

5.

Every module of the form
B_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes B_{k}
, where
B_{i}
is either
U_{r}^{+}
,
U_{r}^{-}
,
U_{2}
,
U_{4}^{+}
,
U_{4}^{-}
,
W_{r^{n}}^{+}
or
W_{r^{n}}^{-}
for
r
prime and where the
B_{i}
have pairwise coprime levels, is simple.

Given
n\geq 0
and
N\geq 1
two non-negative integers, denote by
[n]_{N}
the class of
n
in
\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
. The isomorphism
U_{ab}\rightarrow U_{a}\otimes U_{b}
in Theorem
3.2
(1) sends the vector
e_{[n]_{ab}}
to
e_{[n]_{a}}\otimes e_{[n]_{b}}
. The submodules
U_{p}^{\pm}\subset U_{p}
arising in Theorem
3.2
(3) are spanned by the vectors
e_{i}^{\pm}:=e_{i}\pm e_{-i}
.

The decomposition in simple submodules of
V_{p}(\Sigma_{1})
then follows from the above theorem and from the fact that if
p=2r\geq 6
, the map
\Psi\colon U_{p}^{-}\rightarrow V_{p}(\Sigma_{1})

defined by
\Psi(e_{i}^{-})=u_{i+r-1}
is an isomorphism of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-projective modules. This was proved in
[
3
]
(see also
[
7
, Theorem 5.1]
).

Proof of Lemma
3.1
.

If
p=2r^{2}
with
r
an odd prime, Theorem
3.2
(5) implies that
U_{2r^{2}}^{-}\cong V_{2r^{2}}(\Sigma_{1})
is simple, hence
v_{0}
is cyclic. Assume next that
p=2r_{1}r_{2}
with
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
two distinct odd primes. Theorem
3.2
provides an isomorphism of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
projective modules

\displaystyle\theta\colon\ U_{2r_{1}r_{2}}^{-}\cong\big(U_{2}\otimes U_{r_{1}}^{-}\otimes U_{r_{2}}^{+}\big)\oplus\big(U_{2}\otimes U_{r_{1}}^{+}\otimes U_{r_{2}}^{-}\big).

Observe that both modules
U_{2}\otimes U_{r_{1}}^{-}\otimes U_{r_{2}}^{+}
and
U_{2}\otimes U_{r_{1}}^{+}\otimes U_{r_{2}}^{-}
are simple. To prove that
v_{0}\in V_{2r_{1}r_{2}}(\Sigma_{1})
is cyclic, we need to show that
v:=\theta\circ(\Psi)^{-1}(v_{0})
has non trivial projection in both submodules. First we have
\Psi^{-1}(v_{0})=e_{-1}-e_{1}
(recall that indices are considered modulo
p
). Then we compute:

\displaystyle v=e_{0}\otimes e_{-1}\otimes e_{-1}-e_{0}\otimes e_{1}\otimes e_{1}=\left(\tfrac{1}{2}e_{0}\otimes e_{-1}^{-}\otimes e_{-1}^{+}\right)+\left(\tfrac{1}{2}e_{0}\otimes e_{-1}^{+}\otimes e_{-1}^{-}\right).

The above decomposition shows that projections onto both submodules are non trivial, so
v_{0}
is cyclic.

The case
p=4r
is similar. We start with the decomposition

\displaystyle\theta\colon\ U_{4r}^{-}\cong\big(U_{4}^{-}\otimes U_{r}^{+}\big)\oplus\big(U_{4}^{+}\otimes U_{r}^{-}\big).

Note that the modules on the right-hand side are simple. We keep the notation
v:=\theta\circ(\Psi)^{-1}(v_{0})
and, setting
n=2r-1
, we compute

\displaystyle v=e_{[n]_{4}}\otimes e_{[n]_{r}}-e_{[-n]_{4}}\otimes e_{[-n]_{r}}=e_{1}\otimes e_{-1}-e_{-1}\otimes e_{1}

\displaystyle\hphantom{v}{}=\tfrac{1}{2}(e_{1}-e_{-1})\otimes(e_{1}+e_{-1})-\tfrac{1}{2}(e_{1}+e_{-1})\otimes(e_{1}-e_{-1}).

So the projections of
v_{0}
on the simple submodules of
U_{p}^{-}
are nonzero. This concludes the proof.
∎

3.2
From genus one to higher genus

The fact that the vacuum vector is cyclic in genus one will give us information on the cyclic space of the vacuum vector in higher genus. We now describe the lemma that states this relation.

Choose
\Gamma_{g}\subset{S}^{3}
a banded trivalent graph whose underlying graph has genus
g\geq 2
embedded in the three-sphere. Consider
\Gamma_{g^{\prime}}\subset\Gamma_{g}
a sub-banded graph whose underlying graph has genus
g^{\prime}\leq g
. Let
H_{g}
be a tubular neighborhood of
\Gamma_{g}
and
H_{g^{\prime}}^{1}
,
H_{g^{\prime}}^{2}
two tubular neighborhoods of
\Gamma_{g^{\prime}}
such that
H_{g^{\prime}}^{1}\subset H_{g}\subset H_{g^{\prime}}^{2}
. Denote by
i_{1}\colon H_{g^{\prime}}^{1}\hookrightarrow H_{g}
and
i_{2}\colon H_{g}\hookrightarrow H_{g^{\prime}}^{2}
the embeddings. The embedding
i_{1}
induces a linear map
i_{1}^{*}\colon\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{C}_{g^{\prime}}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{C}_{g}]
sending a framed link
L\subset H_{g^{\prime}}^{1}
to
i_{1}(L)\subset H_{g}
. The morphism
i_{1}^{*}
sends the kernel of the Hopf pairing in genus
g^{\prime}
to a sub-space of the kernel of the Hopf pairing in genus
g
. Hence it induces a linear map
j_{1}\colon V_{p}(\Sigma_{g^{\prime}})\rightarrow V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
. Similarly, the embedding
i_{2}
induces a linear map
j_{2}\colon V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})\rightarrow V_{p}(\Sigma_{g^{\prime}})
. Since
i_{2}\circ i_{1}
is a retraction by deformation, the composition
j_{2}\circ j_{1}
is the identity map of
V_{p}(\Sigma_{g^{\prime}})
.

A framed link
L\subset H_{g}
is called
aligned
if there exist some oriented simple closed curves
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}\subset\Sigma_{g}
such that
\gamma_{1}^{+}\cdot\gamma_{2}^{+}\cdots\gamma_{n}^{+}\cdot\varnothing=L
, where we used the algebra structure of
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{K}_{g}]
and the left-module structure of
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{C}_{g}]
defined at the end of the previous section. Recall that the image of a Dehn twist
\rho_{p}(T_{\gamma})
is equal to the operator
\operatorname{Add}_{p}(\gamma^{+}(\omega))
. It follows that the cyclic space of the vacuum vector is spanned by the vectors
[L(\omega)]
obtained from an aligned framed link by coloring each of its connected components by
\omega
. By construction, the map
i_{1}^{*}
sends aligned links to aligned links.

Fix
\sigma^{\prime}
a
p
-admissible coloring of
\Gamma_{g^{\prime}}
. Consider the
p
-admissible coloring
\sigma
of
\Gamma_{g}
defined by

\displaystyle\sigma(e)=\begin{cases}\sigma^{\prime}(e),&\text{if $e$ is an edge of $\Gamma_{g^{\prime}}\subset\Gamma_{g}$},\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Lemma 3.3
.

If
u_{\sigma^{\prime}}\in V_{p}(\Sigma_{g^{\prime}})
is in the cyclic space of the vacuum vector in genus
g^{\prime}
, then
u_{\sigma}\in V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
also belongs to the cyclic space of the vacuum vector in genus
g
.

Proof of Lemma
3.3
.

By hypothesis, there exists a linear combination of aligned framed links
\mathcal{L}\in\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{C}_{g^{\prime}}]
such that
[\mathcal{L}(\omega)]=u_{\sigma^{\prime}}\in V_{p}(\Sigma_{g^{\prime}})
. We now show that
[i_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{L})(\omega)]=u_{\sigma}\in V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
. Since
i_{1}^{*}
sends aligned framed links to aligned framed links, the claim will follow. Denote by
T_{\sigma^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{C}_{g^{\prime}}]
the element obtained by replacing each edge
e
of
\Gamma_{g}
by the Jones–Wenzl idempotent
f_{\sigma^{\prime}(e)}
and connecting the resulting elements as described in Section
2.1
. By definition, we have
u_{\sigma^{\prime}}=[T_{\sigma^{\prime}}]
and
u_{\sigma}=[j_{1}(T_{\sigma^{\prime}})]
. To prove that the vectors
i_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{L})(\omega)
and
j_{1}(T_{\sigma^{\prime}})
represent the same class in the quotient
V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
, we need to prove that their Hopf pairing with any framed link are equal. Let
K\subset S^{3}\setminus\mathring{H}_{g}
be a framed link. We compute

\displaystyle\big(i_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{L})(\omega),K\big)^{H}_{g,p}=(\mathcal{L}(\omega),j_{2}(K))^{H}_{g^{\prime},p}=(T_{\sigma^{\prime}},j_{2}(K))^{H}_{g^{\prime},p}=(j_{1}(T_{\sigma^{\prime}}),K)^{H}_{g,p},

where we passed from the first line to the second line by using the fact that
[\mathcal{L}(\omega)]=u_{\sigma^{\prime}}=[T_{\sigma^{\prime}}]\in V_{p}(\Sigma_{g^{\prime}})
. This proves that
u_{\sigma}=[i_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{L})(\omega)]
and concludes the proof.
∎

3.3
Cyclicity in genus 2

The goal of this subsection is to prove the following:

Proposition 3.4
.

Suppose that either
p=2r^{2}
or
p=4r
with
r
an odd prime, or
p=2r_{1}r_{2}
with
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
two distinct odd primes. Then the vacuum vector
v_{0}\in V_{p}(\Sigma_{2})
is cyclic.

Let
\Gamma\subset H_{g}
a trivalent graph, as in Section
2
. Two
p
-admissible colorings
\sigma_{1}
,
\sigma_{2}
of
\Gamma
are equivalent if
\mu_{\sigma_{1}(e)}=\mu_{\sigma_{2}(e)}
, for every edges
e\in E(\Gamma)
.

We denote by
\underline{C}_{p}(\Gamma)
the set of equivalence classes of
p
-admissible colorings of
\Gamma
. Given
[\sigma]\in\underline{C}_{p}(\Gamma)
, we associate the subspace

\displaystyle W_{[\sigma]}:=\operatorname{Span}\{u_{\sigma^{\prime}},\,\sigma^{\prime}\in[\sigma]\}\subset V_{p}(\Sigma_{g}).

Lemma 3.5
.

If
X\subset V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
is an invariant
\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g})
-submodule, then
X=\bigoplus_{[\sigma]\in\underline{C}_{p}(\Gamma)}X\cap W_{[\sigma]}
.

Proof.

The matrices
\rho_{p,g}(T_{e})
, for
e\in E(\Gamma)
, generate a commutative subalgebra
\mathcal{T}
of
\mathcal{A}_{p,g}
. To every character
\chi\colon\mathcal{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{*}
, we associate a subspace
V_{p}(\Sigma_{g},\chi)
formed by the vectors
v\in V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
such that
\rho_{p,g}(T_{e})v=\chi(\rho_{p,g}(T_{e}))v
. The set
\underline{C}_{p}(\Gamma)
is in natural bijection with the characters
\chi
such that
V_{p}(\Sigma_{g},\chi)\neq 0
and the spaces
W_{[\sigma]}
correspond to the associated subspaces
V_{p}(\Sigma_{g},\chi)
. Since the orthogonal projector on
X
commutes with the elements of the algebra
\mathcal{T}
, it preserves the subspaces
W_{[\sigma]}
.
∎

The strategy to prove Proposition
3.4
is to apply Lemma
3.5
to the subspace
X:=(\mathcal{A}_{p,2}\cdot v_{0})^{\bot}
which is the orthogonal for the invariant form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{p,g}
of the cyclic space generated by the vacuum vector. Since the invariant form and the Hopf pairing are related by the formula
(v_{1},v_{2})_{p,g}=\langle v_{1},\rho_{p,g}(S)\cdot v_{2}\rangle_{p,g}
, the space
X
is also the orthogonal of the cyclic space
\mathcal{A}_{p,2}\cdot v_{0}
for the Hopf pairing.

Let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}\subset H_{2}
be the framed knots of Fig.
4
. If
a
,
b
,
c
are non-negative integers, define
w_{a,b,c}\in V_{p}(\Sigma_{2})
to be the class of the framed link made of
a
parallel copies of
\gamma_{1}
,
b
parallel copies of
\gamma_{2}
and
c
parallel copies of
\gamma_{3}
.

Figure 4:
The three framed knots defining the vectors
w_{a,b,c}
. The framing is defined by thickening the knots in a surface parallel to the boundary.

Lemma 3.6
.

If
p=4r
, with
r
an odd prime, or if
p=2r_{1}r_{2}
, with
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
two distinct odd primes, then
w_{a,b,c}\in\mathcal{A}_{p,2}\cdot v_{0}
for any
a,b,c\in\{0,1\}
.

Proof.

Lemmas
3.1
and
3.3
imply that
w_{1,0,0}
,
w_{0,1,0}
and
w_{1,1,0}
belong to the cyclic space of the vacuum vector. It remains to show that
w_{1,1,1}
also belongs to this space. Recall that the coefficient
x_{k}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}2&2&k\\
2&2&0\end{array}\right\}
is nonzero when
(2,2,k)
is
p
-admissible.
Using Lemma
2.1
, we have the following system

\displaystyle\hbox to57.88pt{\vbox to34.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 28.93784pt\lower-17.27182pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,,

where
T_{e}
is (a lift of) the Dehn twist around the middle edge of the graph
. Both vectors on the left-hand side belong to the cyclic space of
v_{0}
by Lemmas
3.1
and
3.3
.

Since the coefficients
x_{2}
and
x_{4}
are nonzero and
\mu_{2}\neq\mu_{4}
, the matrix
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x_{2}&x_{4}\\
\mu_{2}x_{2}&\mu_{4}x_{4}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is invertible. We deduce from the above linear system that both vectors
2
2
2
and
2
4
2
belong the cyclic space of the vacuum vector. Since the vector
w_{1,1,1}
belongs to the space spanned by the vectors
2
k
2
for
k=0,2,4
, it belongs the cyclic space of
v_{0}
.
∎

Recall the notation
\mu_{i}=(-1)^{i}A^{i(i+2)}
and the equivalence relation
i\sim j
if
\mu_{i}=\mu_{j}
used to define the spaces
W_{[\sigma]}
. The following lemma describes this equivalence relation.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
i,j\in I_{p}
. Then:

1.

When
p=2r^{2}
with
r
an odd prime, then
\mu_{i}=\mu_{j}
if and only if
i\equiv j\equiv-1\pmod{r}
and
i
,
j
have the same parity.

2.

When
p=4r
with
r
an odd prime, then
\mu_{i}=\mu_{j}
if and only if either
i=j
or
(i=\frac{p-4}{2}-j
and
i
is even
)
.

3.

When
p=2r_{1}r_{2}
with
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
distinct odd primes, then
\mu_{i}=\mu_{j}
if and only if either
i=j
or
j
is the only element satisfying either

\displaystyle\begin{cases}i\equiv j&\pmod{2r_{1}},\\
i\equiv-j-2&\pmod{r_{2}}\end{cases}\qquad\text{or}\qquad\begin{cases}i\equiv j&\pmod{2r_{2}},\\
i\equiv-j-2&\pmod{r_{1}}.\end{cases}

4.

When
p=18
, all
\mu_{i}
are pairwise distinct.

Proof.

The case
p=18
is proved by a straightforward computation. For the other cases, first note that:

\displaystyle\mu_{i}=\mu_{j}\Leftrightarrow A^{p(i+j)+(i-j)(i+j+2)}=1\Leftrightarrow p(i+j)+(i-j)(i+j+2)\equiv 0\pmod{2p}.

(3.1)

When restricted modulo
4
, equation (
3.1
) implies that
i
and
j
have same parity.

When
p=4r
, equation (
3.1
) implies
(i-j)(i+j+2)\equiv 0\pmod{r}
. Since
i\neq j
and
i
,
j
have same parity, then
i=2r-2-j
. When restricted modulo
8
, equation (
3.1
) implies that
i\equiv j\pmod{4}
or
i\equiv j\equiv 0\pmod{2}
. The relations
i=2r-2-j
and
i\equiv j\pmod{4}
forbid
i
and
j
to be odd. Hence
i
and
j
are even and
i=2r-2-j
.

When
p=2r_{1}r_{2}
, equation (
3.1
) implies
(i-j)(i+j+2)\equiv 0\pmod{r_{1}r_{2}}
. Since
i\neq j
and
i
,
j
have same parity, we must have either
i\equiv j\pmod{r_{1}}
and
i\equiv-j-2\pmod{r_{2}}
or
i\equiv j\pmod{r_{2}}
and
i\equiv-j-2\pmod{r_{1}}
.

Finally, when
p=2r^{2}
, equation (
3.1
) implies
(i-j)(i+j+2)\equiv 0\pmod{r^{2}}
. Since
i\neq j
and
i
,
j
have same parity,
r
divides both
i-j
and
i+j+2
and thus
i\equiv j\equiv-1\pmod{r}
.
∎

Lemma 3.8
.

If
p=2r^{2}
, with
r
an odd prime, and
\sigma
is a
p
-admissible coloring of
\Gamma=\hbox to34.54pt{\vbox to34.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 17.27182pt\lower-17.27182pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 23.62 0 C 23.62 13.05 13.05 23.62 0 23.62 C -13.05 23.62 -23.62 13.05 -23.62 0 C -23.62 -13.05 -13.05 -23.62 0 -23.62 C 13.05 -23.62 23.62 -13.05 23.62 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 23.62 L 0 -23.62}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
such that
\sigma(e)\not\equiv-1\pmod{r}
, for all
e\in E(\Gamma)
, then
u_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{A}_{p,2}\cdot v_{0}
. Moreover, if
0\leq a,b,c\leq\frac{r-3}{2}
, then
w_{a,b,c}\in\mathcal{A}_{p,2}\cdot v_{0}
.

Proof.

When
\sigma
satisfies the hypothesis of the lemma, Lemma
3.7
implies that the subspace
W_{[\sigma]}
is one dimensional. Lemma
3.5
implies that this subspace is included either in
\mathcal{A}_{p,2}\cdot v_{0}
, or in its orthogonal. Moreover the Hopf pairing
\left(u_{\sigma},v_{0}\right)^{H}_{p,2}
is nonzero since it is equal to a
p
-admissible
3j
-symbol. Hence we have proved the first statement of the lemma. In particular, we have the inclusion:

\displaystyle S:=\operatorname{Span}\left(\hbox to61.03pt{\vbox to34.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 29.66241pt\lower-17.27182pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 23.62 0 C 23.62 13.05 13.05 23.62 0 23.62 C -13.05 23.62 -23.62 13.05 -23.62 0 C -23.62 -13.05 -13.05 -23.62 0 -23.62 C 13.05 -23.62 23.62 -13.05 23.62 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 23.62 L 0 -23.62}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -23.62 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-26.3294pt}{-2.15277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.36 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-7.03185pt}{-2.15277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{20.60483pt}{-2.15277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},0\leq u,v,w\leq r-2\right)\subset\mathcal{A}_{p,2}\cdot v_{0}.

The proof of the second statement follows from the fact that the vector
w_{a,b,c}
belongs to the subspace
S
whenever we have
a+c\leq r-2
,
b+c\leq r-2
and
a+b\leq r-2
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.4
.

First assume that
p\neq 18
. Consider the graph
\Gamma=\hbox to34.54pt{\vbox to34.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 17.27182pt\lower-17.27182pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 23.62 0 C 23.62 13.05 13.05 23.62 0 23.62 C -13.05 23.62 -23.62 13.05 -23.62 0 C -23.62 -13.05 -13.05 -23.62 0 -23.62 C 13.05 -23.62 23.62 -13.05 23.62 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 23.62 L 0 -23.62}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
, a class
[\sigma]\in\underline{C}_{p}(\Gamma)
, and choose a vector

\displaystyle v\in W_{[\sigma]}\bigcap\left(A_{p,2}\cdot v_{0}\right)^{\bot},\qquad v=\sum_{\tau\in[\sigma]}{\alpha_{\tau}u_{\tau}}.

From Lemma
3.5
, we must show that
v=0
to conclude. To this purpose, we will find
\dim\left(W_{[\sigma]}\right)
linearly independent equations satisfied by the coefficients
\alpha_{\tau}
.

Denote by
F\subset\mathbb{N}^{E(\Gamma)}
the set of functions
f
such that:

•

f(e)\in\{0,1\}
, for all
e\in E(\Gamma)
, if
p=4r
or
p=2r_{1}r_{2}
,

•

f(e)\in\big\{0,\ldots,\frac{r-3}{2}\big\}
, for all
e\in E(\Gamma)
, if
p=2r^{2}
.

Write
E(\Gamma)=\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}
and, given
f\in F
, we define the vector
w_{f}:=w_{f(e_{1}),f(e_{2}),f(e_{3})}
. Lemmas
3.6
and
3.8
imply that
w_{f}\in\mathcal{A}_{p,2}\cdot v_{0}
for all
f\in F
. By definition of
v
, we have that
(w_{f},v)_{p,2}^{H}=0
for all
f\in F
. This implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in[\sigma]}{\left(\prod_{e\in E(\Gamma)}{\lambda_{\tau(e)}^{f(e)}}\right)\alpha_{\tau}\left(u_{\tau},v_{0}\right)_{p,2}^{H}}=0,\qquad\text{for all}\quad f\in F,

where
\lambda_{i}=-\big(A^{2(i+1)}+A^{-2(i+1)}\big)
. Since the complex numbers
(u_{\tau},v_{0})_{p,2}^{H}
are
p
-admissible
3j
symbols, they are nonzero. So it is enough to show that the matrix

\displaystyle M:=\left(\prod_{e\in E(\Gamma)}{\lambda_{\tau(e)}^{f(e)}}\right)_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in[\sigma]\\
f\in F\end{subarray}}

has independent lines.

We now define an invertible square matrix
\widetilde{M}
such that
M
is obtained from
\widetilde{M}
by removing some lines. Given
i\in I_{p}
, we define the set

\displaystyle\omega(i):=\big\{j\in I_{p},\mbox{ so that }\mu_{i}=\mu_{j}\big\}.

Denote by
\#\omega(i)
its cardinal and define the Vandermonde matrix

\displaystyle N[i]:=\big(\lambda_{j}^{n}\big)_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\omega(i)\\
0\leq n\leq\#\omega(i)-1\end{subarray}}.

Since
\lambda_{i}\neq\lambda_{j}
when
i\neq j
, the matrix
N[i]
is invertible. The matrix
\widetilde{M}:=N[e_{1}]\otimes N[e_{2}]\otimes N[e_{3}]
is invertible and
M
is obtained from
\widetilde{M}
by removing the lines corresponding to non
p
-admissible colorings of
\Gamma
. This ends the proof when
p\neq 18
.

The proof of the lemma when
p=18
is similar to Roberts’ proof in
[
12
]
which only relies on the fact that the coefficients
\mu_{i}
are pairwise distinct. We briefly reproduce it. Let
K\subset H_{1}
the framed knot
\{0\}\times S^{1}\subset D^{2}\times S^{1}=H_{1}
with trivial framing, so that
[K]=u_{1}\in V_{p}(\Sigma_{1})
. The Hopf pairing of
K^{i}(\omega)
with
u_{j}
is
(\mu_{j})^{i}
. Since the
\mu_{j}
are pairwise distinct, the Vandermonde matrix
\big(\big(\big[K^{i}(\omega)\big],u_{j}\big)_{18,1}^{H}\big)_{i,j}
is invertible. Since the Hopf pairing is non degenerate, it follows that the vectors
[K^{i}(\omega)]
for
i\in\{0,\ldots,8\}
form a basis of
V_{18}(\Sigma_{1})
. In particular,
u_{1}
is a linear combination of vectors
[K^{i}(\omega)]
. Now choose
L\subset H_{g}
an aligned link. Replacing each connected component of
L
by the above linear combination of parallel copies colored by
\omega
, we see that the class
[L]\in V_{18}(\Sigma_{g})
is equal to the class of a linear combination of aligned links colored by
\omega
, thus belongs to
\mathcal{A}_{18,g}\cdot v_{0}
. Since the vectors
[L]\in V_{18}(\Sigma_{g})
, with
L
aligned, span the whole space
V_{18}(\Sigma_{g})
, this concludes the proof.
∎

4
Decomposition into irreducible factors

In this section, we will prove Theorem
1.1
. Denote by
(\mathcal{A}_{p,g})^{\prime}
the commutant of the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{p,g}
, i.e., the subspace of
\operatorname{End}(V_{p}(\Sigma_{g}))
of operators commuting with all operators
\rho_{p,g}(\phi)
for
\phi\in\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g})
.

The dimension of
(\mathcal{A}_{p,g})^{\prime}
is equal to the number of simple submodules of
V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
. Thus we have to show that
\dim((\mathcal{A}_{p,2})^{\prime})
is one if
p=2r^{2}
and
p=2r_{1}r_{2}
and two when
p=4r
. We will also prove that
\dim((\mathcal{A}_{18,g})^{\prime})=1
.

Consider the linear map
f\colon(\mathcal{A}_{p,g})^{\prime}\hookrightarrow V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
defined by
f(\theta)=\theta\cdot v_{0}
. Since
v_{0}
is cyclic by Proposition
3.4
, the map
f
is injective. Moreover, if
\phi\in\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g})
is the lift of the mapping class of a homeomorphism of
\Sigma_{g}
that extends to
H_{g}
through
\alpha\colon\Sigma_{g}\rightarrow\partial H_{g}
, then
\rho_{p,g}(\phi)\cdot v_{0}=v_{0}
. Denote by
\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(H_{g})\subset\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g})
the subgroup generated by these elements
\phi
. By definition, we have

\displaystyle f((\mathcal{A}_{p,g})^{\prime})\subset\big\{v\in V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})\mbox{ such that }\rho_{p,g}(\phi)\cdot v=v,\mbox{ for all }\phi\in\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(H_{g})\big\}.

In particular, for any trivalent banded graph
\Gamma
, we have the inclusion
f((\mathcal{A}_{p,g})^{\prime})\subset W_{[0]}(\Gamma)
where
[0]
is the class of the coloring sending every edge of
\Gamma
to
0
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
when
p=2r^{2}
and
p=18
.

When
p=2r^{2}
, with
r
an odd prime or
p=18
, then
W_{[0]}
is one-dimensional, generated by
v_{0}
. Hence we have the equalities
(\mathcal{A}_{18,g})^{\prime}=\{\mathds{1}\}
and
(\mathcal{A}_{2r^{2},2})^{\prime}=\{\mathds{1}\}
. Then the Schur lemma proves our claim.
∎

4.1
The case when
\boldsymbol{p=4r}

Assume that
p=4r
with
r
an odd prime and write
k:=2r-2
. By Lemma
3.7
, a color
i\in I_{p}
satisfies
\mu_{i}=1
if and only if either
i=0
or
i=k
. Consider a framed link
L\subset\Sigma_{g}\times\{\frac{1}{2}\}\subset\Sigma_{g}\times[0,1]
, thickened inside
\Sigma_{g}\times\{\frac{1}{2}\}
, and color
L^{p}
by
\omega
. In
[
2
, Section 7]
, it is shown that the operators
\operatorname{Add}_{p}(L^{p}(\omega))
and
\operatorname{Add}_{p}(L(S_{k}))
are equal and only depend on the homology class of
L
in
H_{1}(\Sigma_{g},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
. Hence we have an injective morphism of algebras

\displaystyle i\colon\ \mathbb{C}[H_{1}(\Sigma_{g},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})]\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{p,g}.

The action of a mapping class in homology induces a surjective group morphism
p\colon\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g})\rightarrow\operatorname{Sp}(2g,\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
. By definition of the Witten–Reshetikhin–Turaev representations, we have the following Egorov identity:

\displaystyle\rho_{p,g}(\phi)^{-1}i(w)\rho_{p,g}(\phi)=i(p(\phi)\cdot w),\qquad\!\text{for all}\quad\!\phi\in\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g}),\quad\!w\in\mathbb{C}[H_{1}(\Sigma_{g},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})].\!\!\!

(4.1)

Consider a genus
g
banded trivalent graph
\Gamma
obtained by connecting
g
trivial framed knots by a trivalent banded tree. Such a graph is called a
Lollipop graph
, the
g
edges corresponding to the trivial framed links are called
loop edges
and the edges of the tree are called
trunk edges
. Since
(k,k,k)
is not
p
-admissible, the space
W_{[0]}(\Gamma)
is spanned by the vectors
u_{\sigma}
associated to colorings
\sigma
such that
\sigma(e)=0
if
e
is a trunk edge and
\sigma(e)\in\{0,k\}
if
e
is a loop edge. This basis is in natural bijection with the elements of
H_{1}(H_{g},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
, thus we have a natural isomorphism
\phi\colon\mathbb{C}[H_{1}(H_{g},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})]\cong W_{[0]}(\Gamma)
. Moreover, the isomorphism
\phi
is equivariant for the actions of
\mathbb{C}[H_{1}(\Sigma_{g},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})]
. Precisely, the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}[H_{1}(\Sigma_{g},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})]\times\mathbb{C}[H_{1}(H_{g},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}[H_{1}(H_{g},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i\left(\mathbb{C}[H_{1}(\Sigma_{g},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})]\right)\times W_{[0]}(\Gamma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{[0]}(\Gamma).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i\times\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

We denote by
P
the orthogonal projector of
V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
on the subspace of vectors fixed all operators in
i(\mathbb{C}[H_{1}(\Sigma_{g},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})])
. Clearly
P
belongs to
(\mathcal{A}_{p,g})^{\prime}
.

Consider a symplectic basis
(x_{i},y_{i})_{i=1,\ldots,g}
of
H_{1}(\Sigma_{g},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
, that is classes of curves, still denoted
x_{i}
,
y_{i}
, such that the algebraic intersection of
x_{i}
with
y_{j}
is equal to the Kronecker symbol
\delta_{i,j}
and such that the intersections of
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
with
x_{j}
and
y_{j}
are null when
i\neq j
. We also suppose that the homeomorphism
\alpha\colon\Sigma_{g}\cong\partial H_{g}
, used to define the spaces
V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
in Section
2.2
, sends the curves
x_{i}
to contractible curves in
H_{g}
. Define

\displaystyle\Theta_{i}:=\tfrac{1}{2}(-1+x_{i}+y_{i}+x_{i}y_{i})\in\mathbb{C}[H_{1}(\Sigma_{g},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})].

The
\Theta_{i}
’s pairwise commute, satisfy
\Theta_{i}^{2}=1
and
i(\Theta_{i})\cdot v_{0}
is the vector
u_{\sigma}
associated to the coloring
\sigma
sending the
i
-th loop edge to
k
and other edges to
0
. In particular, writing
\mathcal{W}_{g}:=\mathbb{C}[\Theta_{1},\ldots,\Theta_{g}]\subset\mathbb{C}[H_{1}(\Sigma_{g},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})]
, we have
i(\mathcal{W}_{g})\cdot v_{0}=W_{[0]}(\Gamma)
.

Denote by
I\subset\mathbb{C}[H_{1}(\Sigma_{g},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})]
the ideal generated by the elements
(x_{i}-1)
for
1\leq i\leq g
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Consider the action of
\operatorname{Sp}(2g,\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
on
i(\mathbb{C}[H_{1}(\Sigma_{g},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})])
. Then:

1.

The subspace of vectors fixed by
\operatorname{Sp}(2g,\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
is
\operatorname{Span}(\mathds{1},P)
.

2.

For every
w\in\mathcal{W}_{g}
and
\phi\in\operatorname{Sp}(2g,\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
, we have
\phi\cdot w-w\in I
.

Proof.

The first point follows from the well-known fact that the action of
\operatorname{Sp}(2g,\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
on
H_{1}(\Sigma_{g},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
has two orbits: the singleton containing the neutral element and the set containing the other elements. To prove the second point, denote by
X_{i}
,
Y_{i}
,
Z_{i,j}
for
1\leq i,j\leq g
the classes in
H_{1}(\Sigma_{g},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
of the Dehn twists of Fig.
5
generating
\operatorname{Sp}(2g,\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
. We suppose that
X_{i}
and
Y_{i}
represent the classes of Dehn twists along curves whose homology classes are
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
respectively oriented such that
X_{i}\cdot y_{i}=x_{i}y_{i}
and
Y_{i}\cdot x_{i}=x_{i}y_{i}
. First note that the elements
\Theta_{i}
are invariant under the action of the
X_{j}
and
Y_{j}
. We are reduced to show that for any
w\in\mathcal{W}_{g}
, we have
Z_{i,j}\cdot w-w\in I
. We make the proof for the generator
Z_{1,2}
, the other cases are similar.

Figure 5:
The oriented curves defining some Dehn twists whose homology classes generate
\operatorname{Sp}(6,\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
.

First note that
Z_{1,2}\cdot\Theta_{i}=\Theta_{i}
when
i\notin\{1,2\}
. Then we compute:

\displaystyle Z_{1,2}\cdot\Theta_{1}-\Theta_{1}=\tfrac{1}{2}y_{1}(1+x_{1})(x_{1}x_{2}-1)\in I,

\displaystyle Z_{1,2}\cdot\Theta_{2}-\Theta_{2}=\tfrac{1}{2}y_{2}(1+x_{2})(x_{1}x_{2}-1)\in I.

∎

The case
p=4r
of Theorem
1.1
follows from the

Proposition 4.2
.

If
p=4r
, with
r
an odd prime, is such that
v_{0}\in V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
is cyclic, then
V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
is the direct sum of two simple submodules.

Proof.

Consider the linear map
h\colon\mathcal{W}_{g}\rightarrow W_{[0]}(\Gamma)
defined by
h(w)=i(w)\cdot v_{0}
. The map
h
is surjective and its kernel is the ideal
I
. Lemma
4.1
(2) and equation (
4.1
) imply that

\displaystyle\rho_{p,g}(\phi)\circ i(w)\cdot v_{0}=i(w)\circ\rho_{p,g}(\phi)\cdot v_{0},\qquad\text{for all}\quad\phi\in\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g}),\quad w\in\mathcal{W}_{g}.

Let
\theta\in(\mathcal{A}_{p,g})^{\prime}
. Since
\theta\cdot v_{0}
lies in
W_{[0]}(\Gamma)
and
h
is surjective, there exists an element
w\in\mathcal{W}_{g}
such that
i(w)\cdot v_{0}=\theta\cdot v_{0}
. Moreover, if
\phi\in\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g})
, then

\displaystyle\theta\circ\rho_{p,g}(\phi)\cdot v_{0}=\rho_{p,g}(\phi)\circ\theta\cdot v_{0}=\rho_{p,g}(\phi)\circ i(w)\cdot v_{0}=i(w)\circ\rho_{p,g}(\phi)\cdot v_{0}.

The cyclicity of
v_{0}
implies that
\theta=i(w)\in i(\mathcal{W}_{g})
, hence we have the inclusion
\left(\mathcal{A}_{p,g}\right)^{\prime}\subset i(\mathcal{W}_{g})\subset i\left(\mathbb{C}[H_{1}(\Sigma_{g},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})]\right)
.

Moreover, Lemma
4.1
(1) implies that
(\mathcal{A}_{p,g})^{\prime}\cap i(\mathbb{C}[H_{1}(\Sigma_{g},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})])=\operatorname{Span}(\mathds{1},P)
. Hence we have the equality
(\mathcal{A}_{p,g})^{\prime}=\operatorname{Span}(\mathds{1},P)
which proves the claim.
∎

4.2
The case when
\boldsymbol{p=2r_{1}r_{2}}

Assume that
p=2r_{1}r_{2}
with
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
distinct odd primes. In this case, there exists an unique integer
x\in\{1,\ldots,r_{1}r_{2}-2\}
such that
\mu_{x}=1
. Then
x
is even and verifies either

\displaystyle\begin{cases}x\equiv-2&\pmod{r_{1}},\\
x\equiv 0&\pmod{r_{2}},\end{cases}\qquad\text{or}\qquad\begin{cases}x\equiv 0&\pmod{r_{1}},\\
x\equiv-2&\pmod{r_{2}}.\end{cases}

We begin by stating a technical lemma whose proof will be the subject of the next subsection:

Lemma 4.3
.

If
(x,x,x)
is
p
-admissible and
r_{1},r_{2}>37
, then we have the following inequality

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}x&x&2\\
x&x&0\end{array}\right\}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}x&x&4\\
x&x&x\end{array}\right\}\neq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}x&x&4\\
x&x&0\end{array}\right\}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}x&x&2\\
x&x&x\end{array}\right\}.

Lemma 4.4
.

Suppose
p=2r_{1}r_{2}
, with
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
distinct odd primes, such that either
(x,x,x)
is not
p
-admissible, or
r_{1},r_{2}>37
, then

\displaystyle W_{[0]}(\Gamma_{1})\bigcap W_{[0]}(\Gamma_{2})\subset\operatorname{Span}(v_{0},b_{1},b_{2}).

Proof.

The subspaces
W_{[0]}(\Gamma)
are spanned by the vectors associated to colorings of the edges of
\Gamma
by the elements
0
and
x
. If
(x,x,x)
is not
p
-admissible, then these colorings of
\Gamma_{2}
correspond to the elements
v_{0}
,

,

and the proof is immediate. Suppose

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

Lemma 4.5
.

There exists an element
a\in\mathcal{A}_{2r_{1}r_{2},1}
such that

\displaystyle a\cdot u_{0}=u_{x},\qquad a\cdot u_{x}=u_{0}.

Proof.

It is enough to show that there exists an operator
\psi\in(\mathcal{A}_{2r_{1}r_{2},1})^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle\psi\cdot u_{0}=u_{x}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\psi\cdot u_{x}=u_{0}.

The cyclicity of
u_{0}
, provided by Lemma
3.1
, implies the existence of
a\in\mathcal{A}_{2r_{1}r_{2},1}
such that

a\cdot u_{0}=u_{x}
. If such a
\psi
exists, then

\displaystyle a\cdot u_{x}=a\circ\psi\cdot u_{0}=\psi\circ a\cdot u_{0}=u_{0}.

The operator
\psi
is defined as follows. Given
i\in\{0,\ldots,r_{1}r_{2}-2\}
, only one of the following two cases occurs:

1.

Either there exists
j\in\{0,\ldots,r_{1}r_{2}-2\}
so that

\displaystyle\begin{cases}j\equiv i&\pmod{2r_{1}},\\
j\equiv-i-2&\pmod{r_{2}},\end{cases}

and we set
\psi(u_{i}):=u_{j}
.

2.

Or there exists
j\in\{0,\ldots,r_{1}r_{2}-2\}
so that

\displaystyle\begin{cases}j\equiv i&\pmod{2r_{2}},\\
j\equiv-i-2&\pmod{r_{1}},\end{cases}

and we set
\psi(u_{i}):=-u_{j}
.

A straightforward computation shows that
\psi
commutes with
\rho_{p,1}(T)
and
\rho_{p,1}(S)
and either
\psi
or
-\psi
sends
u_{0}
to
u_{x}
.
∎

We define two operators
a\otimes\mathds{1},\mathds{1}\otimes a\in\mathcal{A}_{p,2}
as follows. Consider
\Gamma_{2},\Gamma^{\prime}_{2}\subset S^{3}
the two entangled genus
2
banded graphs of Fig.
6
. Denote by
\Gamma_{1}\subset\Gamma_{2}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}\subset\Gamma^{\prime}_{2}
the genus one subgraphs of the left picture of Fig.
6
. Fix
H_{2},H^{\prime}_{2}
some tubular neighborhood of
\Gamma_{2}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}_{2}
respectively which do not intersect and
H_{1}\subset H_{2}
,
H^{\prime}_{1}\subset H^{\prime}_{2}
some tubular neighborhoods of
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}
respectively. Identify the closure of
S^{3}\setminus(H_{i}\cup H^{\prime}_{i})
with
\Sigma_{i}\times[0,1]
and
V_{p}(\Sigma_{i})
with the space of linear combinations of framed links in
H_{i}
quotiented by the kernel of the Hopf pairing induced by the Heegaard splitting
S^{3}=H_{i}\cup\overline{(S^{3}\setminus H_{i})}
. From Lemma
4.5
, there exists some
a\in\mathcal{A}_{p,1}
such that
a\cdot u_{x}=u_{0}
and
a\cdot u_{0}=u_{x}
. Let
\mathcal{L}
be a linear combination of aligned links in
\overline{S^{3}\setminus(H_{1}\cup H^{\prime}_{1})}\cong\Sigma_{1}\times[0,1]
such that
\operatorname{Add}_{p,1}\left(\mathcal{L}(\omega)\right)=a
. Composing eventually by an isotopy, we can suppose that the framed parallel links of
\mathcal{L}
are in
\overline{S^{3}\setminus(H_{2}\cup H^{\prime}_{2})}\cong\Sigma_{2}\times[0,1]
. We denote by
a\otimes\mathds{1}:=\operatorname{Add}_{p,2}\left(\mathcal{L}(\omega)\right)\in\mathcal{A}_{p,2}
the resulting operator. Note that this definition is not canonical since it depends on the choice of
a
and on how the middle edges of
\Gamma_{2}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}_{2}
are entangled with the framed links of
\mathcal{L}
. Nevertheless, by definition this operator satisfies
(a\otimes\mathds{1})\cdot v_{0}=b_{1}
and
(a\otimes\mathds{1})^{2}\cdot v_{0}=v_{0}
. Similarly, by considering the genus one subgraphs of
\Gamma_{2}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}_{2}
of the right picture of Fig.
6

such that

and

. We further suppose that the aligned framed links defining these two operators are not entangled so the two operators
a\otimes\mathds{1}
and

commute. We eventually define

which satisfies
(a\otimes a)\cdot v_{0}=\hbox to51.62pt{\vbox to28.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 8.7359pt\lower-8.7359pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

Proof of Theorem
1.1
when
p=2r_{1}r_{2}
.

Recall we defined a linear map
f\colon(\mathcal{A}_{p,2})^{\prime}\hookrightarrow V_{p}(\Sigma_{2})
by
f(\theta)=\theta\cdot v_{0}
and that the image
f\left((\mathcal{A}_{p,2})^{\prime}\right)\subset W_{[0]}(\Gamma_{2})
is invariant under the orientation-preserving homeomorphisms of the handlebody
H_{2}
. Using Lemma
4.4
and the fact that the vectors in the image of
f
must be invariant under a homeomorphism permuting the two handles of
H_{2}

\displaystyle f((\mathcal{A}_{p,2})^{\prime})\subset\operatorname{Span}(v_{0},b_{1}+b_{2}).

f((\mathcal{A}_{p,2})^{\prime})=\mathbb{C}\cdot v_{0}
. By contradiction, suppose that there exists

Thus
\theta^{2}\cdot v_{0}
does not belong to
\operatorname{Span}(v_{0},b_{1}+b_{2})
. This contradicts the fact that
\theta^{2}\in(\mathcal{A}_{p,2})^{\prime}
.
∎

4.3
Proof of Lemma
4.3

In this subsection we consider
p=2r_{1}r_{2}
, with
r_{1},r_{2}>37
two distinct odd primes. We suppose that there exists
x\in\{1,\ldots,r_{1}r_{2}-2\}
so that
(x,x,x)
is
p
-admissible and

\displaystyle\begin{cases}x\equiv 0&\pmod{2r_{1}},\\
x\equiv-2&\pmod{r_{2}}.\end{cases}

We also choose some primitive
r_{1}
-th and
r_{2}
-th roots of unity
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
, such that
A^{2}=A_{1}A_{2}
. In particular, we have
A^{2x}=A_{2}^{-2}
.

The goal of this subsection is to show that

\displaystyle D:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}x&x&2\\
x&x&0\end{array}\right\}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}x&x&4\\
x&x&x\end{array}\right\}-\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}x&x&4\\
x&x&0\end{array}\right\}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}x&x&2\\
x&x&x\end{array}\right\}\neq 0.

A straightforward computation, using the formula of the recoupling coefficients
[
10
]
, gives

\displaystyle D=(-1)^{\frac{x}{2}+1}\frac{[3][5]![x]\left[\frac{3}{2}x+1\right]!(\left[\frac{x}{2}\right]!)^{3}}{[2][x+3]!([x+2]!)^{2}[x+3]\left[\frac{x}{2}+1\right]}\left(\left[\frac{x}{2}-1\right]^{2}\left[\frac{x}{2}\right]^{2}\left[\frac{x}{2}+1\right]\right.

\displaystyle\hphantom{D =}{}-[2]^{2}\left[\frac{x}{2}\right]^{3}\left[\frac{3}{2}+2\right]\left[\frac{x}{2}+1\right]+\left[\frac{x}{2}-1\right]\left[\frac{x}{2}\right]\left[\frac{x}{2}+1\right]\left[\frac{x}{2}+2\right]\left[\frac{x}{2}+3\right]

\displaystyle\left.\hphantom{D=}{}+[x-1][x+3]\left[\frac{x}{2}\right]^{2}\left[\frac{x}{2}+1\right]-[x-1]\left[\frac{3}{2}x+2\right][x+3]\right)

\displaystyle\hphantom{D}{}=(-1)^{\frac{x}{2}+1}\frac{[3][5]![x]\left[\frac{3}{2}x+1\right]!(\left[\frac{x}{2}\right]!)^{3}}{[2][x+3]!([x+2]!)^{2}[x+3]\left[\frac{x}{2}+1\right](A^{2}-A^{-2})^{7}A_{1}^{10}A_{2}^{9}}\cdot P(A_{1},A_{2}),

where
P
is defined by

\displaystyle P(x,y):=x^{20}y^{16}-x^{17}y^{17}-x^{16}y^{18}+x^{19}y^{13}-4x^{18}y^{14}+3x^{17}y^{15}+2x^{15}y^{17}-x^{19}y^{11}

\displaystyle\hphantom{P(x,y):=}{}-5x^{17}y^{13}-4x^{15}y^{15}+4x^{14}y^{16}-2x^{13}y^{17}-x^{18}y^{10}+2x^{17}y^{11}+6x^{16}y^{12}

\displaystyle\hphantom{P(x,y):=}{}+2x^{15}y^{13}-x^{14}y^{14}+2x^{13}y^{15}+x^{11}y^{17}+2x^{15}y^{11}+x^{14}y^{12}+x^{13}y^{13}-6x^{12}y^{14}

\displaystyle\hphantom{P(x,y):=}{}+x^{10}y^{16}+x^{17}y^{7}+4x^{16}y^{8}-x^{15}y^{9}-4x^{14}y^{10}+4x^{12}y^{12}+x^{15}y^{7}+x^{13}y^{9}

\displaystyle\hphantom{P(x,y):=}{}-4x^{12}y^{10}+x^{11}y^{11}+4x^{10}y^{12}-4x^{8}y^{14}-x^{7}y^{15}-2x^{15}y^{5}-6x^{14}y^{6}-4x^{13}y^{7}

\displaystyle\hphantom{P(x,y):=}{}+2x^{12}y^{8}-8x^{11}y^{9}-6x^{10}y^{10}-6x^{9}y^{11}-x^{7}y^{13}+x^{13}y^{5}+6x^{11}y^{7}+6x^{10}y^{8}

\displaystyle\hphantom{P(x,y):=}{}+8x^{9}y^{9}-2x^{8}y^{10}+4x^{7}y^{11}+6x^{6}y^{12}+2x^{5}y^{13}+x^{13}y^{3}+4x^{12}y^{4}-4x^{10}y^{6}

\displaystyle\hphantom{P(x,y):=}{}-x^{9}y^{7}+4x^{8}y^{8}-x^{7}y^{9}-x^{5}y^{11}-4x^{8}y^{6}+4x^{6}y^{8}+x^{5}y^{9}-4x^{4}y^{10}-x^{3}y^{11}

\displaystyle\hphantom{P(x,y):=}{}-x^{10}y^{2}+6x^{8}y^{4}-x^{7}y^{5}-x^{6}y^{6}-2x^{5}y^{7}-x^{9}y-2x^{7}y^{3}+x^{6}y^{4}-2x^{5}y^{5}

\displaystyle\hphantom{P(x,y):=}{}-6x^{4}y^{6}-2x^{3}y^{7}+x^{2}y^{8}+2x^{7}y-4x^{6}y^{2}+4x^{5}y^{3}+5x^{3}y^{5}+xy^{7}-2x^{5}y

\displaystyle\hphantom{P(x,y):=}{}-3x^{3}y^{3}+4x^{2}y^{4}-xy^{5}+x^{4}+x^{3}y-y^{2}.

Note that
P
does not depend neither on
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
nor on
x
. We have to show that
P(A_{1},A_{2})\neq 0
. By contradiction, suppose that
P(A_{1},A_{2})=0
and define
H(X):=P(X^{r_{2}},X^{r_{1}})\in\mathbb{Z}[X]
. Since
P
has integral coefficients the formula
P(A_{1},A_{2})=0
holds for any roots of unity
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
of order
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
respectively. In particular, we have
H(A^{2})=0
and
H(X)
is divisible by the product
\phi_{r_{1}}(X)\phi_{r_{2}}(X)
of the two cyclotomic polynomials of order
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
. Thus its degree satisfies
\deg(H)\geq(r_{1}-1)(r_{2}-1)
. Using the above formula for
P
we deduce that

\displaystyle(r_{1}-1)(r_{2}-1)\leq\deg(H)=\begin{cases}20r_{2}+16r_{1},&\text{if}\ 2r_{2}\geq r_{1},\\
16r_{2}+18r_{1},&\text{if}\ 2r_{2}\leq r_{1},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\Leftrightarrow\begin{cases}(r_{1}-21)(r_{2}-17)\leq 356,&\text{if}\ 2r_{2}\geq r_{1},\\
(r_{1}-17)(r_{2}-19)\leq 352,&\text{if}\ 2r_{2}\leq r_{1}.\end{cases}

Since
r_{1},r_{2}>37
are prime, then
r_{1},r_{2}\geq 41
. We conclude by noticing that none of the two equations of the above system does have solutions satisfying
r_{1},r_{2}\geq 41
.

5
Partial generalizations in higher genus

The main obstruction to extend the above techniques in genus
g\geq 3
lies in the proof of Proposition
3.4
. To prove the cyclicity of the vacuum vector, we found linear independent equations satisfied by any vector of
W_{[\sigma]}\bigcap\left(A_{p,g}\cdot v_{0}\right)^{\bot}
and deduced that this space must be trivial. The non-triviality of these equations followed from the fact that the Hopf pairing
(u_{\sigma},v_{0})^{H}_{p,2}
is a
3j
-symbol, thus does not vanish. In higher genus, for a suitable choice of trivalent graph, this Hopf pairing is a product of
6j
-symbols, which might a priori vanish. Indeed, suppose
p=2(k+2)
with
k\geq 2
an even integer, and consider the two following families of
6j
-symbols:

where
a+b+c\equiv 2\pmod{4}

where
a+\frac{c+k}{2}\equiv 1\pmod{2}

Proposition 5.1
.

The
6j
-symbols of type I and type II, defined above, vanish.

We postpone the technical proof of Proposition
5.1
to Appendix
A
. We conjecture that the
6j
-symbols of type I and II are the only vanishing
6j
-symbols:

Conjecture 5.2
.

If
4
divides
p
, then the only vanishing
6j
-symbols at level
p
are the two families type I and type II from above. If
4
does not divide
p
, there is no vanishing
6j
-symbol at level
p
.

Numerical computations permitted us to verify the validity of the conjecture up to level
66
. Motivated by this conjecture, we will show the following:

Proposition 5.3
.

1.

Let
p=2r_{1}r_{2}
with
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
distinct odd primes such that either
r_{1},r_{2}>37
or the element
x
defined in the introduction satisfies
3x>2r_{1}r_{2}-2
. Suppose that
p
verifies Conjecture
5.2
. Then
V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
is simple for arbitrary
g\geq 3
.

2.

Let
p=4r
with
r
an odd prime and suppose that
p
verifies Conjecture
5.2
. Then
V_{p}(\Sigma_{3})
is the direct sum of two simple modules.

Theorem
1.2
is a consequence of this proposition and the computer assisted verification of Conjecture
5.2
up to level
66
. To prove Proposition
5.3
, we first show that the arguments of section
4
generalize:

Lemma 5.4
.

Suppose that
p,g\geq 3
are such that
v_{0}\in V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
is cyclic, then:

1.

If
p=2r_{1}r_{2}
with
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
distinct odd primes such that either
r_{1},r_{2}>37
or
3x>2r_{1}r_{2}-4
, then
V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
is simple.

2.

If
p=4r
with
r
an odd prime, then
V_{4r}(\Sigma_{g})
is the sum of two simple modules.

Proof.

The case
p=4r
follows from Proposition
4.2
. Suppose that
p=2r_{1}r_{2}
is such that
v_{0}\in V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
is cyclic and recall that there exists an unique color
x\neq 0
such that
\mu_{x}=1
.

Step 1:
Suppose that
(x,x,x)
is
p
-admissible and
r_{1},r_{2}>37
. Let
\Gamma_{1}
,
\Gamma_{2}
be two trivalent graphs which only differ by a single Whitehead move inside a ball
B^{3}
, as drawn in Fig.
3
. Then

\displaystyle W_{[0]}(\Gamma_{1})\cap W_{[0]}(\Gamma_{2})\subset\operatorname{Span}\big(u_{\sigma}^{\Gamma_{1}},\ \text{such that}\ \sigma(a)\sigma(b)\sigma(c)\sigma(d)=0\big).

Indeed, let
\sigma_{1}
,
\sigma_{2}
be two
p
-admissible colorings of
\Gamma_{1}
, with colors
0
or
x
, such that

\sigma_{1}(e)=\sigma_{2}(e)
, for all
e\in E(\Gamma_{1})\setminus\{i\}
and with
\sigma_{i}(a)=\sigma_{i}(b)=\sigma_{i}(c)=\sigma_{i}(d)=x
and
\sigma_{1}(i)=0
,
\sigma_{2}(i)=x
.

Suppose that there exists
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
such that the vector
v:=\alpha u_{\sigma_{1}}+\beta u_{\sigma_{2}}\in W_{[0]}(\Gamma_{2})
. Then, as in the proof of Lemma
4.4
, using fusion-rules, we show that
(\alpha,\beta)
satisfies a system of two linear equations which are linearly independent by Lemma
4.3
. Hence we have
v=0
.

Step 2:
If
i\in\{1,\ldots,g\}
, we denote by
b_{i}\in V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
the vector representing a single framed knot colored by
x
around the
i
-th hole. We have:

\displaystyle\bigcap_{\Gamma}W_{[0]}(\Gamma)=\operatorname{Span}(v_{0},b_{i},1\leq i\leq g),

where the intersection is over the set of embedded banded trivalent graphs of genus
g
.

Indeed, let
\Gamma
represents the graph of Fig.
7
and
\sigma
a
p
-admissible coloring such that:

1)

\sigma(e)\in\{0,x\}
, for all
e\in E(\Gamma)
,

2)

there exists
i<j
with
\sigma(a_{i})=\sigma(b_{i})=\sigma(a_{j})=\sigma(b_{j})=x
.

We can suppose that for every
i<k<j
, we have
\sigma(a_{k})\sigma(b_{k})=0
.

Step
1
with
a=a_{i}
,
b=a_{j}
,
c=b_{i}
and
d=b_{j}
implies that the projection of
u_{\sigma}
on
\bigcap_{\Gamma}W_{[0]}(\Gamma)
vanishes. We conclude by noticing that if
\sigma
is a
p
-admissible coloring of
\Gamma
, with colors in
\{0,x\}
, that does not satisfy the condition 2, then either
u_{\sigma}=b_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,\ldots,g\}
or else
u_{\sigma}=v_{0}
.

Figure 7:
A trivalent banded graph of genus
g
.

Step 3:
We conclude the proof as in the case
g=2
. Using Step
2
and the fact that the vectors of the image of
f
must be invariant under permutation of the handles, we have the inclusion:

f((\mathcal{A}_{p,g})^{\prime})\subset\operatorname{Span}(v_{0},b_{1}+\dots+b_{g})
. We must show that
f\left((\mathcal{A}_{p,g})^{\prime}\right)=\mathbb{C}\cdot v_{0}
. By contradiction, suppose that there exists
\theta\in(\mathcal{A}_{p,g})^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle\theta\cdot v_{0}=b_{1}+\cdots+b_{g}=(a\otimes\mathds{1}\otimes\!\cdots\!\otimes\mathds{1}+\mathds{1}\otimes a\otimes\mathds{1}\otimes\!\cdots\!\otimes\mathds{1}+\dots+\mathds{1}\otimes\!\cdots\!\otimes\mathds{1}\otimes a)\cdot v_{0},

where
a\otimes\mathds{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathds{1}
is defined using Lemma
4.5
as in the
g=2
case. We have

\displaystyle\theta^{2}\cdot v_{0}=\big(a\otimes\mathds{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathds{1}+\dots+\mathds{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathds{1}\otimes a\big)^{2}\cdot v_{0}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\theta^2 \cdot v_0}{}=gv_{0}+2(a\otimes a\otimes\mathds{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathds{1})\cdot v_{0}+\dots+2(\mathds{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathds{1}\otimes a\otimes a)\cdot v_{0}.

Hence
\theta^{2}\cdot v_{0}
does not belong to
\bigcap_{\Gamma}W_{[0]}(\Gamma)
. This contradicts the fact that
\theta^{2}\in(\mathcal{A}_{p,g})^{\prime}
.
∎

We now extend Proposition
3.4
to higher genus. A genus
g\geq 2
trivalent banded graph
\Gamma_{g}
is called a
fly-eyes-graph
if it is obtained from
\Gamma_{2}=\hbox to34.54pt{\vbox to34.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 17.27182pt\lower-17.27182pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 23.62 0 C 23.62 13.05 13.05 23.62 0 23.62 C -13.05 23.62 -23.62 13.05 -23.62 0 C -23.62 -13.05 -13.05 -23.62 0 -23.62 C 13.05 -23.62 23.62 -13.05 23.62 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 23.62 L 0 -23.62}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
by a sequence of
g-2
operations which consist in choosing an arbitrary vertex and inserting a triangle as drawn in the left part of Fig.
8
. The right part of Fig.
8
provides an example of a genus
8
fly-eyes-graph. For instance, the Lollipop graphs defined in Section
4.1
are not fly-eyes-graphs. There is only one genus
3
fly-eyes-graph which is the tetrahedron graph. It follows from standard fusion-rules that if
\sigma
is a
p
-admissible coloring of a fly-eyes-graph, then the Hopf pairing
(u_{\sigma},v_{0})_{p,g}^{H}
is a product of
6j
-symbols multiplied by some non-vanishing constant.

Figure 8:
On the left, the operation transforming a fly-eyes-graph of genus
g
into a one of genus
g+1
. On the right, an example of a genus
8
fly-eyes-graph.

Fix
g\geq 3
and embed a fly-eyes-graph
\Gamma_{g}
in
S^{3}
. Denote by
H_{g}
the embedded handlebody
H_{g}:=S^{3}\backslash\mathring{N}(\Gamma_{g})
where
N(\Gamma_{g})
denotes a tubular neighborhood of
\Gamma_{g}
. For each edge
e\in E(\Gamma_{g})
, fix a curve
\gamma_{e}\subset H_{g}
which bounds a disc intersecting
\Gamma_{g}
only once along
e
. We construct a map

\displaystyle w\colon\ {\mathbb{N}}^{E(\Gamma_{g})}\rightarrow V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})

as follows. To
f\colon E(\Gamma_{g})\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
we associate the class in
V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
of the framed link consisting of
f(e)
parallel copies of
\gamma_{e}
, thickened along
\Sigma_{g}
, for each edge
e\in E(\Gamma_{g})
.

Lemma 5.5
.

If
p=4r
, with
r
an odd prime, or
p=2r_{1}r_{2}
, with
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
distinct odd primes, then
w_{f}\in\mathcal{A}_{p,g}\cdot v_{0}
for all
f\in\{0,1\}^{E(\Gamma_{g})}
.

Proof.

We will show the stronger result that if
\sigma
is a
p
-admissible coloring such that there exists an edge
e
satisfying
\sigma(e)=1
, then
u_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{A}_{p,g}\cdot v_{0}
. The conclusion of the lemma will follow. Fix an edge
e\in E(\Gamma_{g})
and denote by
{}_{i}\Sigma(\gamma_{e})_{i}
the surface obtained by cutting
\Sigma_{g}
along
\gamma_{e}
and gluing back two discs with one framed puncture colored by
i
. By the colored splitting
[
2
, Theorem 1.4]
, we have a canonical isomorphism

\displaystyle V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})\cong\oplus_{i}V_{p}(_{i}\Sigma(\gamma_{e})_{i}).

Denote by
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g-1,2})
the mapping class group of a genus
g-1
surface with two boundary components and by
\rho_{p,g}^{i}\colon\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g-1,2})\rightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V_{p}(_{i}\Sigma(\gamma_{e})_{i}))
the Witten–Reshetikhin–Turaev representation associated to
{}_{i}\Sigma(\gamma_{e})_{i}
(see
[
2
]
for definitions). There is a natural embedding
\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g-1,2})\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g})
such that the following diagram commutes for every color
i
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g-1,2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{GL}(V_{p}(_{i}\Sigma(\gamma_{e})_{i}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\operatorname{Mod}}(\Sigma_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{GL}(V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{p,g}^{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{p,g}}

It follows from the main theorem of
[
6
]
that for
i=1
, the representation
\rho_{p,g}^{1}
is irreducible. Lemmas
3.1
and
3.3
imply that there exists a
p
-admissible coloring
\sigma
with
\sigma(e)=1
and
u_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{A}_{p,g}\cdot v_{0}
. The irreducibility of
\rho_{p,g}^{1}
implies that the cyclic space of
u_{\sigma}
contains the whole subspace
V_{p}(_{1}\Sigma(\gamma_{e})_{1})
. This subspace is spanned by the vectors
u_{\sigma}
such that
\sigma(e)=1
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Suppose that
p=4r
and consider the genus
3
tetrahedron graph
\Gamma
. A
p
-admissible coloring
\sigma
of
\Gamma
is said of type I or II if the corresponding
6j
-symbol
\left<u_{\sigma}\right>
is of type I or II respectively. Denote by
Z_{p,3}\subset V_{p}(\Sigma_{3})
the subspace generated by the vectors
u_{\sigma}
associated to
p
-admissible colorings of the tetrahedron graph which are neither of type I nor of type II.

Lemma 5.6
.

1.

If
g\geq 3
,
p=2r_{1}r_{2}
with
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
distinct odd primes, and if
p
verifies Conjecture
5.2
, then
v_{0}\in V_{p}(\Sigma_{g})
is cyclic.

2.

If
p=4r
, with
r
an odd prime, verifies Conjecture
5.2
, then
Z_{p,3}\subset\mathcal{A}_{p,3}\cdot v_{0}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the genus
2
case. Choose a vector

\displaystyle v\in W_{[\sigma]}\bigcap(A_{p,g}\cdot v_{0})^{\bot}v=\sum_{\tau\in[\sigma]}{\alpha_{\tau}u_{\tau}}.

If
p=4r
, we further suppose that
v\in Z_{p,3}
. We must find
\dim\left(W_{[\sigma]}\right)
independent equations verified by the coefficients
\alpha_{\tau}
. Denote by
F\subset\mathbb{N}^{E(\Gamma)}
the set of functions
f
such that
f(e)\in\{0,1\}
for all
e\in E(\Gamma)
. Lemma
5.5
implies that
w_{f}\in\mathcal{A}_{p,g}\cdot v_{0}
for all
f\in F
. By definition of
v
, we have that
\left(w_{f},v\right)_{p,g}^{H}=0
for all
f\in F
. Thus we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in[\sigma]}{\left(\prod_{e\in E(\Gamma)}{\lambda_{\tau(e)}^{f(e)}}\right)\alpha_{\tau}(u_{\tau},v_{0})_{p,g}^{H}}=0,\qquad\text{for all}\quad f\in F.

Since the complex numbers
(u_{\tau},v_{0})_{p,g}^{H}
are nonzero, it is enough to show that the matrix
M:=\Big(\prod\limits_{e\in E(\Gamma)}{\lambda_{\tau(e)}^{f(e)}}\Big)_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in[\sigma]\\
f\in F\end{subarray}}
has independent lines to conclude the proof. Recall that we defined Vandermonde matrices
N[e]
in the proof of Proposition
3.4
. Denote by
e_{1},\ldots,e_{3g-3}
the edges of
\Gamma
. The matrix
M
is obtained from the invertible matrix
N[e_{1}]\otimes\cdots\otimes N[e_{3g-3}]
by removing the lines corresponding to non
p
-admissible colorings of
\Gamma
, so has independent lines. This concludes the proof.
∎

To complete the proof of Proposition
5.3
, it remains to show that if
p=4r
, with
r
an odd prime, verifies Conjecture
5.2
and
\sigma
is a coloring of the tetrahedron graph of type I or II, then
u_{\sigma}
belongs to the cyclic subspace generated by
v_{0}\in V_{p}(\Sigma_{3})
. We cut the proof in three technical lemmas.

Lemma 5.7
.

Suppose
p=4r
with
r\geq 7
an odd prime. Let
\sigma
be a coloration of the tetrahedron graph which is

1)

either of type
I
and such that either we have
(a\neq\frac{k}{2})
or
(b\neq\frac{k}{2})
,

2)

or of type
II
and such that either we have
(a\neq b
and
a\neq k-b)
or
c\equiv\frac{k}{2}\pmod{4}
,

then
u_{\sigma}
belongs to the cyclic space generated by
v_{0}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar in both cases and relies on the following remark: embed a colored tetrahedron graph in
H_{3}
, choose two opposite edges of the graph and perform two Whitehead moves on these edges. We get this way another embedding of the tetrahedron graph inside
H_{3}
. While choosing the edges colored by
b
and its opposite colored by
\frac{k}{2}
in a type
I
coloration of the tetrahedron graph, we have

\displaystyle\hbox to57.49pt{\vbox to57.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 29.22328pt\lower-22.96185pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

If
i=\frac{k}{2}
then
\alpha_{i,j}=0
. If
i\neq\frac{k}{2}
then the vector
j
a
\frac{k}{2}
b
i
\frac{k}{2}
belongs to
Z_{p,3}
, since either
a\neq\frac{k}{2}
or
b\neq\frac{k}{2}
. Hence this vector belongs to the cyclic space generated by
v_{0}
by Lemma
5.6
. This concludes the proof in the first case. The second case is proved similarly.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

Suppose

with

an odd prime. Let

be a coloration of the tetrahedron graph of type

is a vector orthogonal to the first one.

Proposition
3.4
implies that the vacuum vector is cyclic in genus
2
and Lemma
3.3
implies that the vector
0
a
\frac{k}{2}
\frac{k}{2}

belongs to the cyclic space generated by the vacuum vector in genus
3
.

When
a=\frac{k}{2}
, then
W_{[v_{a}]}
is one dimensional, so according to Lemma
3.5
, either
v_{\frac{k}{2}}
belongs the cyclic space generated by
v_{0}
or it belongs to its orthogonal. Since its scalar product with the vector

0
a
\frac{k}{2}
\frac{k}{2}

is a non null
3j
-symbol, the vector
v_{a}
is in the cyclic space of
v_{0}
.

When
a\neq\frac{k}{2}
, then
W_{[v_{a}]}
is two dimensional generated by
v_{a}
and
v_{k-a}
. If
v=\alpha_{1}v_{a}+\alpha_{2}v_{k-a}
belongs to the orthogonal of the cyclic space generated by
v_{0}
, then
v
is orthogonal to both vectors

0
a
\frac{k}{2}
\frac{k}{2}
and

0
k-a
\frac{k}{2}
\frac{k}{2}
. This implies that
v=0
so
W_{[v_{a}]}
is included in the cyclic space of the vacuum vector.
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

Suppose
p=4r
with
r\geq 7
an odd prime. Let
\sigma
be a coloration of the tetrahedron graph of type
II

(
either

or
a=k-b)
and
(c\equiv\frac{k}{2}+2\pmod{4})
. Then
u_{\sigma}
belongs to the cyclic space generated by
v_{0}
.

Applying the preceding Whitehead move in the opposite direction, we see that
\rho_{p,3}(T)\cdot u_{\sigma}
belongs to the space generated by the vectors of the form
j
a
k{-}a
a
c
a
. Whenever
j\neq\frac{k}{2}
, these generating vectors belong to
Z_{p,3}
and thus to the cyclic space generated by the vacuum vector. Denote by
\beta
the scalar product
\langle u_{\sigma},\rho_{p,3}(T)u_{\sigma}\rangle
.

If
\beta=0
, then
\rho_{p,3}(T)\cdot u_{\sigma}
belongs to the cyclic space generated by
v_{0}
, so does
u_{\sigma}
since
\rho_{p,3}(T)
is invertible.

If
\beta\neq 0
, then the operator
A:=\beta\cdot\mathds{1}+\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}a&a&\frac{k}{2}\\
k-a&a&\frac{k}{2}\end{array}\right\}\rho_{p,3}(T)\in\mathcal{A}_{p,3}
is invertible since
\rho_{p,3}(T)
has finite order. Since
A\cdot u_{\sigma}
belongs to the cyclic space generated by the vacuum vector, so does
u_{\sigma}
.
∎

Then we have
F(a,b)=(-1)^{\frac{b+k}{2}+a}
.

Proof.

A straightforward computation using the formulas of
[
10
]
gives
F(a,b)=\frac{f(a)}{g(b)}
, where
f(a)=(-1)^{a}[a+1]![k-a]!
and
g(b)=(-1)^{\frac{k+b}{2}}\left[\frac{k-b}{2}\right]!\left[\frac{k+b}{2}+1\right]!
. Note that

\displaystyle\frac{f(a+1)}{f(a)}=-\frac{[a+2]}{[k-a]}=-1\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{g(b+2)}{g(b)}=-\frac{\left[\frac{k+b}{2}+2\right]}{\left[\frac{k-b}{2}\right]}=-1.

We conclude using the fact that
F\big(\frac{k}{2},2\big)=-1
.
∎

and
